[www.bloomberg.com/apps/news?pid=newsarchive&sid=aUXYrK3i6juc](http://www.bloomberg.com/apps/news?pid=newsarchive&sid=aUXYrK3i6juc).

**38<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Lloyd
Blankfein, was in the room](#Chapter2.html_ch2note16):** Mark Pittman,
“Goldman, Merrill Collect Billions After Fed’s AIG Bailout Loans,”
Bloomberg News, September 29, 2008,
[www.bloomberg.com/apps/news?pid=newsarchive&sid=aTzTYtlNHSG8](http://www.bloomberg.com/apps/news?pid=newsarchive&sid=aTzTYtlNHSG8).

**39<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[It’s
funded by its member banks](#Chapter2.html_ch2note17):** That’s to say,
the twelve regional branches of the Federal Reserve sell shares to the
community banks within their jurisdictions.

**39<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[twice
a year for a proper grilling](#Chapter2.html_ch2note18):** The
Humphrey-Hawkins Act of 1978 mandated that the Federal Reserve chairman
appear in front of the relevant committees of the House and Senate in
February and July of every year to discuss monetary policy.

**39<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[that
can hit “CTRL+P” whenever it wants](#Chapter2.html_ch2note19):** The
Federal Reserve creates money.

**39<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[The
Fed deserves its reputation for secrecy](#Chapter2.html_ch2note20):**
Once again, my sources are Greider, *Secrets of the Temple*, and
Griffin, *The Creature from Jekyll Island*.

**42<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Neither
were the banks’ official reports](#Chapter2.html_ch2note21):** Frank
Partnoy and Jesse Eisinger, “What’s Inside America’s Banks?” *Atlantic*,
January 2, 2013,
[www.theatlantic.com/magazine/archive/2013/01/whats-inside-americas-banks/309196](http://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/archive/2013/01/whats-inside-americas-banks/309196).

**43<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[a
sham, a fake empire](#Chapter2.html_ch2note22):** One of Pittman’s
favorite bands was The National, which has a song called “Fake Empire.”

**43<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[a way
for Bernanke to cover Geithner’s ass](#Chapter2.html_ch2note23):**
Pittman was stunned when President-elect Barack Obama chose Geithner as
his Treasury secretary. I called it the Obama administration’s original
sin.

**44<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[infamous
plunge of the stock market](#Chapter2.html_ch2note24):** The Dow Jones
Industrial Average lost 7 percent of its value, or about \$1.2 trillion,
on September 29, 2008, as the House of Representatives defeated the TARP
proposal 205–228. Four days later TARP passed.

**44<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[growing
disconnect between ordinary people](#Chapter2.html_ch2note25):** I
realize this is a minority sentiment, but later events would show that
the <span id="Notes.html_page_214"></span>stock market and the “real
economy” were functioning as if on different planets.

**44<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[increasing
the money supply is bound to cause rising
prices](#Chapter2.html_ch2note26):** This is indeed fundamental
macroeconomics, but the money that’s created needs to flow through the
system, and in this case it oozed. More on the velocity of money in
Chapter 3.

**46<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[nine
Freedom of Information Act exemptions](#Chapter2.html_ch2note27):** Here
they are:

<span class="hide">              </span>1. Those documents properly
classified as secret in the interest of national defense or foreign
policy;

<span class="hide">              </span>2. Related solely to internal
personnel rules and practices;

<span class="hide">              </span>3. Specifically exempted by
other statutes;

<span class="hide">              </span>4. A trade secret or privileged
or confidential commercial or financial information obtained from a
person;

<span class="hide">              </span>5. A privileged inter-agency or
intra-agency memorandum or letter;

<span class="hide">              </span>6. A personnel, medical, or
similar file the release of which would constitute a clearly unwarranted
invasion of personal privacy;

<span class="hide">              </span>7. Compiled for law enforcement
purposes, the release of which

<span class="hide">                  </span>a. could reasonably be
expected to interfere with law enforcement proceedings,

<span class="hide">                  </span>b. would deprive a person of
a right to a fair trial or an impartial adjudication,

<span class="hide">                  </span>c. could reasonably be
expected to constitute an unwarranted invasion of personal privacy,

<span class="hide">                  </span>d. could reasonably be
expected to disclose the identity of a confidential source,

<span class="hide">                  </span>e. would disclose
techniques, procedures, or guidelines for investigations or
prosecutions, or

<span class="hide">                  </span>f. could reasonably be
expected to endanger an individual’s life or physical safety;

<span class="hide">              </span>8. Contained in or related to
examination, operating, or condition reports about financial
institutions that the SEC regulates or supervises; or

<span id="Notes.html_page_215"></span><span class="hide">              </span>9.
And those documents containing exempt information about gas or oil
wells.

<span class="hide">           </span>*Source:* “Freedom of Information
Act Exemptions,” Securities and Exchange Commission,
[www.sec.gov/foia/nfoia.htm](http://www.sec.gov/foia/nfoia.htm).

**46<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[had
borrowed \$20, \$30, even \$40 for every
dollar](#Chapter2.html_ch2note28):** Cyrus Sanati, “Cayne and Schwartz
at Odds over Bear’s Leverage,” *New York Times*, May 5, 2010,
<http://dealbook.nytimes.com/2010/05/05/cayne-and-schwartz-at-odds-over-bears-leverage/?_r=0>.

**47<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Founder
Michael Bloomberg had hired Winkler](#Chapter2.html_ch2note29):**
Winkler told this anecdote at a newsroom gathering to commemorate the
twentieth anniversary of Bloomberg News. For more on the genesis of
Bloomberg News, see Michael R. Bloomberg, *Bloomberg by Bloomberg* (New
York: John Wiley & Sons, 1997).

**48<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Bloomberg
. . . was asked if he was okay with the
lawsuit](#Chapter2.html_ch2note30):** Peter Grauer, the Bloomberg LP
chairman, asked the company’s majority owner for permission to proceed
with the lawsuit. The mayor gave it.

**48<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[one
of subpoena envy](#Chapter2.html_ch2note31):** Would you rather (1) be
able to fly, (2) possess super strength, or (3) have subpoena power?

**49<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[broomstick
of the Wicked Witch of the West](#Chapter2.html_ch2note32):** Dorothy’s
quest in Victor Fleming’s *The Wizard of Oz*, a film about the gold
standard.

**49<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Bloomberg
LP filed suit](#Chapter2.html_ch2note33):** “to compel the Fed to
discharge its obligations under FOIA, so that the public can be informed
of how the Fed is safeguarding its money.” See *Bloomberg L.P. v. Board
of Governors of the Federal Reserve System: Filing: 1*, Case number
1:2008cv09595, [Justia.com](http://Justia.com),
<http://docs.justia.com/cases/federal/district-courts/new-york/nysdce/1:2008cv09595/335178/1>.

**50<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[stepped
on the fingers of the economy](#Chapter2.html_ch2note34):** Hat tip to
characters played by Cary Grant and Martin Landau in Alfred Hitchcock’s
*North by Northwest*.

**50<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[This
is how the FOIA often works](#Chapter2.html_ch2note35):** It is, in
fact, how it did work. Though I set up this anecdote as a hypothetical,
all the facts are accurate.

**51<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Brad

Scene—‘The Box,’” You Tube,

Hat tip to Scott Raab.

<span id="Notes.html_page_216"></span>**52<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Joseph
Heller’s World War II novel *Catch-22*](#Chapter2.html_ch2note37):** For
fans of irony, just about the best thing ever. Not the best book. The
best *thing* ever.

**52<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Federal
Reserve processed 857 FOIA requests](#Chapter2.html_ch2note38):** I cite
2010 statistics because that was the year I received 1,000 double-sided
pages of redactions from the central bank. See the Fed’s annual FOIA
reports on the Federal Reserve website at
[www.federalreserve.gov/foia/annualreports.htm](http://www.federalreserve.gov/foia/annualreports.htm).

**53<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Called
the Valukas report](#Chapter2.html_ch2note39):** The report by Anton R.
Valukas, chairman of the Chicago-based law firm Jenner & Block. A more
meticulous account of an investment bank in its death throes has yet to
be written. Finance geeks rejoice: “Lehman Brothers Holdings Inc.
Chapter 11 Proceedings Examiner Report,” Jenner & Block,
<http://jenner.com/lehman>.

**53<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Obama

**56<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Republican
Scott Garrett](#Chapter2.html_ch2note47):** Full disclosure: Garrett was

saw me standing on the curb, he waved at me and said, “Hi Bob!” My
neighbors were impressed. Their dogs quit pooping on my lawn for a whole
week.

Zumbrun and Jeff Kearns, “Fed Maintains \$85 Billion Pace of Purchases
as Growth Pauses,” Bloomberg News, January 30, 2013,
[www.bloomberg.com/news/2013–01–30/fed-maintains-85-billion-pace-of-purchases-as-growth-pauses.html](http://www.bloomberg.com/news/2013–01–30/fed-maintains-85-billion-pace-of-purchases-as-growth-pauses.html).

**70<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[They
call it the velocity of money](#Chapter3.html_ch3note6):** The Federal
Reserve Bank of St. Louis defines it as “the number of times one dollar
is spent to buy goods and services per unit of time.” Researchers at the
bank track it, and the graph line goes, unskiably, straight down the
mountain during the recession of 2007 to 2009 and then stays at the
chalet on the bottom. See for yourself here: “Velocity of M2 Money Stock
(M2V),” FRED Economic Data, September 26, 2013,
<http://research.stlouisfed.org/fred2/series/M2V>.

**71<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[US
economy was recovering](#Chapter3.html_ch3note7):** Fry and Taylor, “An
Uneven Recovery, 2009–2011.”

**71<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[longest
post–World War II recession](#Chapter3.html_ch3note8):** The National
Bureau of Economic Research compiles recession statistics. It says the
2007–2009 recession, at eighteen months, beat 1973 to 1975 and 1981 to
1982, which were each sixteen months long. The Great Depression was
forty-three months long, followed by one month of growth, then another
thirteen months of contraction. Ouch. But nothing in American history
compares to 1873 to 1879, with sixty-five straight months of economic
contraction. See “US Business Cycle Expansions and Contractions,”
National Bureau of Economic Research,
[www.nber.org/cycles.html](http://www.nber.org/cycles.html).

**72<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[The
Fed did all it could](#Chapter3.html_ch3note9):** Some critics say the
Federal Reserve ought to have sat on its hands and let the free market
determine winners and losers. I prefer to shy away from judgments about
the wisdom of the Fed’s policies and concentrate on their effects. But I
will say this: the bailouts of 2008 and 2009 had already tossed
<span id="Notes.html_page_222"></span>dirt on the corpse of free market
capitalism. Postcrisis, we were in untrammeled territory.

**72<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[0.25
percent interest](#Chapter3.html_ch3note10):** The Federal Reserve’s
press release is clear as mud: “Interest on Required Balances and Excess
Balances,” Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System,
[www.federalreserve.gov/monetarypolicy/reqresbalances.htm](http://www.federalreserve.gov/monetarypolicy/reqresbalances.htm).

**72<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Citigroup
had \$107.2 billion](#Chapter3.html_ch3note11):** The amount banks hold
in reserve at the Federal Reserve is tallied on the FDIC website at
[www2.fdic.gov/IDASP/main.asp](http://www2.fdic.gov/IDASP/main.asp).
Thanks to Dakin Campbell of Bloomberg News for his guidance.

**72<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[Billy
Dunavant](#Chapter3.html_ch3note13):** Interviewed at his Memphis home
in June 2012. His favorite expression was “Is a lady pig made of pork?”
He said it every time I asked a dumb question, which was, according to
my transcript of the conversation, only two or three times a minute.

**73<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[“Helicopter
Ben” Bernanke](#Chapter3.html_ch3note14):** The Federal Reserve chairman
got stuck with this nickname when he advocated the injection of cash
into the financial system to combat falling prices indicative of a
depression. Economist Milton Friedman coined the phrase “helicopter
drop” of money in this context. I would surmise that a crop-dusting
airplane would do the trick too, but “Crop-Dusting Airplane Ben” is not
a catchy nickname.

**73<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[7
million](#Chapter3.html_ch3note15) (foreclosures):** Gopal and Benson,
“American Dream Erased.”

**73<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[ZIRP
was terrific for home owners](#Chapter3.html_ch3note16):** The interest
rate for a thirty-year fixed-rate home loan fell to 3.31 percent, its
lowest ever, in November 2012, according to government-controlled
mortgage buyer Freddie Mac.

**73<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[12
million at the 2011 peak](#Chapter3.html_ch3note17):** Not the most
exact of calculations. Estimates from real estate data collectors
CoreLogic, RealtyTrac, and Zillow have varied, positing that from
one-quarter to one-third of home owners owe more on their mortgages than
their homes are worth. See Jordan Weissmann, “Millennial Homeowners Are
Especially <span id="Notes.html_page_223"></span>Screwed,” *Atlantic
Cities*, August 27, 2012,
[www.theatlanticcities.com/housing/2012/08/why-millenial-homeowners-are-underwater/3086](http://www.theatlanticcities.com/housing/2012/08/why-millenial-homeowners-are-underwater/3086).

**91<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[“That’s
why I’m richer than you”](#Chapter3.html_ch3note69):** Media coverage of
this exchange disappointed me because it was mostly pro-Dimon and
usually included the observation that Mayo was an unpopular bank
analyst. In the video clip from Bloomberg TV, commentator Adam Johnson
at least puts Mayo’s supposed lack of popularity into context—if Mayo
was indeed unpopular, it was because he told the truth and refused to
kowtow to bank brass. This is exactly the type of analyst investors
should listen to, imho. See “Jamie Dimon to Analyst: ‘That’s Why I’m
Richer Than You,’” YouTube,
[www.youtube.com/watch?feature=player_embedded&v=8G871csJS6Q#at=11](http://www.youtube.com/watch?feature=player_embedded&v=8G871csJS6Q#at=11).

**98<span class="hide">       </span><span class="spacen2"></span>[less

Financial Stability Board proposed additional “loss absorbency” in the
form of capital, <span id="Notes.html_page_233"></span>bank by bank. The
four banks that the board would require to fund themselves with the most
cash were Citigroup, HSBC, Deutsche Bank, and JPMorgan Chase. See
“Update of Group of Global Systemically Important Banks (G-SIBs),”
Financial Stability Board, November 1, 2012,
[www.financialstabilityboard.org/publications/r_121031ac.pdf](http://www.financialstabilityboard.org/publications/r_121031ac.pdf).

executive director for financial stability, were ahead of their American
counterparts on this issue.

Financial Services Roundtable sent fast facts: “Fast Facts: Dodd-Frank
Cumulative Weight,” Financial Services Roundtable,
[www.fsround.org/fsr/pdfs/fast-facts/ff-2013–03–15-Dodd-Frank-Cumulative-Weight.pdf](http://www.fsround.org/fsr/pdfs/fast-facts/ff-2013–03–15-Dodd-Frank-Cumulative-Weight.pdf).
Financial Services Forum sent a statement from its chief executive
officer, Rob Nichols:

<span class="hide">                   </span>Thanks to a wide array of
legislative, industry and regulatory reforms—including increased capital
requirements and Dodd-Frank—the U.S. financial system is more safe,
sound and stable that ever before. The financial services industry
strongly believes that no institution should be “too big to fail” and
taxpayer dollars should not be available to bail out a failed firm. As
policymakers implement financial services reforms or suggest additional
measures, it is critical that any new policy optimizes economic growth
and job creation—as well as maintaining America’s position as the global
economic leader.

<span class="hide">             </span>The Clearing House Association
made its chief economist, Bob Chakravorti, available for an interview,
in which he said, “Many <span id="Notes.html_page_236"></span>people
believe that anything a big bank does can be done by a smaller bank. We
don’t believe that. Some of the things can be done, but at a much higher
cost.” Sounds reasonable, but when I asked him where the savings accrued
by big banks went, he couldn’t answer.

**106<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[added
costs of complying with postcrisis
regulations](#Chapter4.html_ch4note28):** Bank of America estimated the
cost of complying with Dodd-Frank at about \$2 billion over the first
two years.

**107<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[throwing
a new committee at it](#Chapter4.html_ch4note29):** Dodd-Frank did away
with the Office of Thrift Supervision, which oversaw savings and loans
(S&Ls)—or at least was supposed to. The two biggest S&Ls in the United
States, Washington Mutual and IndyMac, both bit the dust in the
financial crisis. But Dodd-Frank also established the Financial
Stability Oversight Council, chaired by the Treasury secretary, which
has the power to kill a dying institution. Dodd-Frank also left the
details of implementation to the various bureaucracies.

**109<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[offer
a different way](#Chapter4.html_ch4note30):** “Brown, Vitter Unveil
Legislation That Would End ‘Too Big to Fail’ Policies,” Sherrod Brown,
Senator for Ohio, April 24, 2013,
[www.brown.senate.gov/newsroom/press/release/brown-vitter-unveil-legislation-that-would-end-too-big-to-fail-policies](http://www.brown.senate.gov/newsroom/press/release/brown-vitter-unveil-legislation-that-would-end-too-big-to-fail-policies).
Brown, a progressive Democrat, and Vitter, a conservative Republican,
offer a great example of how issues around too big to fail cut across
party and ideological boundaries.

**109<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[Bear
Stearns was operating](#Chapter4.html_ch4note31):** Sanati, “Cayne and
Schwartz at Odds.”

not be authorized (i) to own, operate, or invest in facilities for the
extraction, storage or transportation of commodities.” See the Fed’s
November 18, 2005, press release at the Federal Reserve website at
[www.federalreserve.gov/boarddocs/press/orders/2005/20051118](http://www.federalreserve.gov/boarddocs/press/orders/2005/20051118).

**112<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[JPMorgan
Chase bought Henry Bath & Son](#Chapter5.html_ch5note2):** Andrew

<span id="Notes.html_page_237"></span>of RBS Sempra,” Bloomberg News,
February 16, 2010,
[www.bloomberg.com/apps/news?pid=newsarchive&sid=aSDKaUvU_BaA](http://www.bloomberg.com/apps/news?pid=newsarchive&sid=aSDKaUvU_BaA).
Henry Bath & Son was part of JPMorgan Chase’s acquisition of RBS Sempra.

**112<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[Other
regulatory agencies](#Chapter5.html_ch5note3):** I spoke to Stephanie

Currency. Both said they had no knowledge of a waiver.

**113<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[a
dusty section of the 1934 Securities and Exchange
Act](#Chapter5.html_ch5note4):** I refer to Section 13(b)(3)(A).

**113<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[to
conduct secret operations](#Chapter5.html_ch5note5):** John C. Coffee, a
Columbia University securities law professor, explained what the law
could cover to *Businessweek* reporter Dawn Kopecki: “What you might
hide is investments: You’ve spent umpteen million dollars that comes out
of your working capital to build a plant in Iraq,” which the government
wants to keep secret. “That’s the kind of scenario that would be
plausible,” Coffee said.

**117<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[prices
for industrial metals collapsed](#Chapter5.html_ch5note13):** The price
of aluminum on the spot market went from \$3,271.25 per metric ton on
July 11, 2008, to \$1,251.75 on February 23, 2009, according to data
compiled by Bloomberg News. That’s a 62 percent nosedive in seven
months.

**117<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[a
situation traders call “contango”](#Chapter5.html_ch5note14):** London
Metal Exchange prices for aluminum 3, 15, 27, 63, and 123 months into
the future were all higher than the spot price in July 2013.

**133<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[Zelma
Lacey, a widow](#Chapter6.html_ch6note1):** Interview with Walter Lacey
and Marianne Miller-Lacey. Details were confirmed with the court
document “In re: Walter W. Lacey, Debtor; Walter W. Lacey, Plaintiff, v.
BAC Home Loans Servicing and Others,” available on Google Scholar, at
<http://scholar.google.com/scholar_case?case=13373418248947727307&q=lacey+feeney&hl=en&as_sdt=2,22>.

**134<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[It
was bizarre](#Chapter6.html_ch6note2):** Yet the pattern was repeated
for years all over the country.

**135<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[As
suicidally as the mortgage industry](#Chapter6.html_ch6note3):** They
were, to put it indelicately, shitting their own bed. Neighbors directly
affect each other’s home values.

**135<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[any
of us could have fallen behind](#Chapter6.html_ch6note4):** A lost job
or illness causes most mortgage delinquencies. The role of moral
turpitude is exaggerated.

**136<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[*Lord
of the Flies* territory](#Chapter6.html_ch6note5):** William Golding,
*Lord of the Flies* (1954). British schoolboys survive a plane crash on
a remote, uninhabited island and create their own society without
adults. Kind of like *Lost* without “Freckles.”

**136<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[Keystone
Cops coming to the “rescue”](#Chapter6.html_ch6note6):** Named after the
film studio for whom they romped, the Keystone Cops were bumbling law
enforcement officers played for laughs in silent movies of the 1910s.

**136<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[Five
Stooges](#Chapter6.html_ch6note7):** One representing each of Wall
Street’s five families of mortgage servicing.

**136<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[like
Franz Kafka’s Gregor Samsa](#Chapter6.html_ch6note8):** Franz Kafka,
*The Metamorphosis* (1915). The protagonist wakes up one morning as a
cockroach.

**138<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[About
half the mortgage companies](#Chapter6.html_ch6note9):** This was the
estimate of David Olson, a former director of market research at Freddie
Mac who ran his own mortgage consultant shop and had been in the
industry for more than fifty years.

**139<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[The
Laceys received the letter](#Chapter6.html_ch6note10):** Details from
the court document “In re: Walter W. Lacey, Debtor” on Google Scholar.

<span id="Notes.html_page_243"></span>**140<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[launched
HAMP to great fanfare](#Chapter6.html_ch6note11):** The initiative came
with video testimonials, supportive blogs, and its own website. Just a
month into his first term, Obama presented the outlines of the program
in a speech in Mesa, Arizona: “Remarks by the President on the Home
Mortgage Crisis,” White House, February 18, 2009,
[www.whitehouse.gov/the-press-office/remarks-president-mortgage-crisis](http://www.whitehouse.gov/the-press-office/remarks-president-mortgage-crisis).

**140<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[1.1
million home owners . . . More than one-third of home
owners](#Chapter6.html_ch6note12):** Clea Benson, “Treasury Extends HAMP

**142<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[A

members in April 2010, just after the passage of Obamacare, according to
Rasmussen, a national polling company. That figure declined to 8 percent
in January 2013 as the outrage faded. “Just 8% Now Say They Are Tea
Party Members,” *Rasmussen Reports*, January 7, 2013,
[www.rasmussenreports.com/public_content/politics/general_politics/january_2013/just_8_now_say_they_are_tea_party_members](http://www.rasmussenreports.com/public_content/politics/general_politics/january_2013/just_8_now_say_they_are_tea_party_members).

**146<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[SPOC—a
single point of contact](#Chapter6.html_ch6note22):** The settlement
monitor believed the SPOC was essential too. From the
frequently-asked-questions section of the National Mortgage Settlement
website:

<span class="hide">                 </span>The banks have agreed to
major reforms in how they service mortgage loans. These new servicing
standards require lenders and servicers to adhere to a long list of
rights for those facing foreclosure. For example, borrowers will have
the right to see all of their loan documents to make sure any potential
foreclosure is legal; they will be given every opportunity to first
modify their loan before facing foreclosure; lenders and servicers will
be required to have <span id="Notes.html_page_245"></span>an appropriate
number of well-trained staff members to promptly respond to the needs of
distressed borrowers; and finally, borrowers will have the right to deal
with a reliable, single point of contact so they have access to a person
from whom to obtain information throughout the process. This is very
important because, throughout the foreclosure crisis, borrowers have
lodged widespread complaints about their frustrations in trying to work
with their lenders. They’ve complained about unresponsive employees,
lost documents, and conflicting information. (“Joint State-Federal
Mortgage Servicing Settlement FAQ,” National Mortgage Settlement,
[www.nationalmortgagesettlement.com/faq](http://www.nationalmortgagesettlement.com/faq).)

**146<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[faulted
the servicers for what they called severe
deficiencies](#Chapter6.html_ch6note23):** “Foreclosure Review:
Opportunities Exist to Further Enhance Borrower Outreach Efforts,” US
Government Accountability Office, June 2012,
[www.gao.gov/assets/600/592059.pdf](http://www.gao.gov/assets/600/592059.pdf).
The optimistic title belies a savaging of the process.

There were two Senate hearings on the foreclosure review that April.
Warren’s questioning occurred at the April 11, 2013, hearing of the
Senate Banking Committee’s Financial Institutions and Consumer
Protection Subcommittee, chaired by Sherrod Brown, an Ohio Democrat. The
panel featured executives from three of the foreclosure review
contractors: Promontory, Deloitte, and PricewaterhouseCoopers.

17, 2013, was called by the Housing, Transportation, and Community
Development Subcommittee, chaired by New Jersey Democrat Robert
Menendez. Witnesses included Lawrence L. Evans of the US Government
Accountability Office, National Mortgage Settlement monitor Joseph A.
Smith Jr., and Deborah Goldberg of the National Fair Housing Alliance.

**155<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[He
told the New York City Bar Association](#Chapter6.html_ch6note55):**
Lanny Breuer addressed the New York City Bar Association on September
13, 2012. This is the relevant section from his speech:

<span id="Notes.html_page_249"></span><span class="hide">                   </span>The
decision whether to indict a corporation, defer prosecution or decline
altogether is not one that I . . . \[take\] lightly. We are frequently
on the receiving end of presentations from defense counsel, CEOs and
economists . . . who argue that the collateral consequences of an
indictment would be devastating, just devastating for their client. In
my conference room over these past years, I’ve heard sober predictions
that a company or bank might fail if we indict. That innocent employees
could lose their jobs. That entire industries could be affected, and
that even global markets will feel the effects. Sometimes, but let me
stress not always, these presentations are compelling. In reaching every
charging decision we must take into account the effect of an indictment
on innocent employees and shareholders, just as we must take into
account the nature of the crimes committed and the pervasiveness of the
misconduct.

<span class="hide">                   </span>I personally feel that it’s
my duty to consider whether individual employees with no responsibility
for or knowledge of misconduct committed by others in the same company
are going to lose their livelihoods if we indict the corporation. In
large multinational companies, the jobs of ten of thousands of employees
can literally be at stake, and in some cases the health of an industry
or the markets are a very real factor. Those are the kinds of
considerations in white-collar cases that literally keep me awake at
night, and which must—must—play a role in responsible enforcement. (“The
Role of Deferred Prosecution Agreements in White Collar Criminal Law
Enforcement,” YouTube,
[www.youtube.com/watch?v=1gbcB5BRzXo](http://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1gbcB5BRzXo))

<span class="hide">             </span>As far as I can tell, Breuer made
no mention of any sleeplessness due to the results of the suspicious
behavior of large multinational companies, which can also harm people
who are innocent.

<span class="hide">             </span>Thanks to Martin Smith and the
staff at *Frontline* for the tip.

upward slightly. “Graph: Compensation of Employees: Wages & Salary
Accruals (WASCUR)/Gross Domestic Product (GDP),” FRED Economic Data,

**162<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[one
in four residents lived below the poverty
line](#Chapter7.html_ch7note10):** In Memphis, 27.2 percent of people
lived below the federal poverty line in 2011, compared with a national
poverty rate of 15 percent, according to the US Census Bureau. In 2010,
the poverty threshold was \$22,314 for a family of four. A few more
poverty statistics: 15.1 percent—just over 46 million Americans—were
officially in poverty in 2010. This is an increase from 12.5 percent in
2007. Among racial and ethnic groups, African Americans had the highest
poverty rate, 27.4 percent, followed by Hispanics at 26.6 percent and
whites at 9.9 percent; 45.8 percent of young black children (under age
six) lived in poverty, compared to 14.5 percent of white children.

**167<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[blitz
of TV commercials](#Chapter7.html_ch7note29):** Ally aimed to showcase
what it called its “exceptional customer service.” In one commercial, it
replaced an employee at a dry cleaner with a blender—and customers
didn’t like it! They wanted to talk to a person, not a blender! Older
commercials showed an actor being indulgent to one child and mean to
another. The indulgent treatment was ostensibly how Ally dealt with its
customers; the mean way, presumably, was its competitors’ method. The
December 12, 2011, announcement about the new batch of commercials is at
“Ally Bank Launches Two New ‘People Sense’ Commercials,” Ally,
<http://media.ally.com/index.php?s=20295&item=122855>. The blender
commercial is at “Ally Bank TV Spot, ‘Dry Cleaner Test: Blender,’”
iSpot.tv,
[www.ispot.tv/ad/7wtI/ally-bank-dry-cleaner-test-blender](http://www.ispot.tv/ad/7wtI/ally-bank-dry-cleaner-test-blender).

**168<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[purple
martins prefer beetles](#Chapter7.html_ch7note31):** Herbert W. Kale II,
“The Relationship of Purple Martins to Mosquito Control,” Florida
Audubon Society, 1968, <http://purplemartin.org/update/MosCont.html>.
The study concluded that

<span class="hide">                   </span>mosquitoes appear to be a
negligible item in the diet of the purple martin. . . . None of the
published statements appearing in the popular or ethnological literature
that attribute a mosquito-feeding habit to the purple martin are based
on a factual study; the oft-quoted statement ‘a martin eats 2,000
mosquitoes per day’ has no evident means of support. . . . The purple
martin is one of our most beautiful and friendly birds. It daily
consumes a large number of insects. Its aesthetic qualities alone
recommend it highly to man. There is no need to ascribe to the martin
abilities greater than those it already possesses in order to encourage
its protection and propagation.

**172<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[Martin
Luther King Jr. and Elvis Presley](#Chapter7.html_ch7note35):** King was
from Atlanta, of course. He was thirty-nine years old when he was shot
and killed on the balcony of the Lorraine Motel in Memphis on April 4,
1968. The motel has been converted into a spellbinding civil rights
museum. At the end of the self-guided tour, visitors find themselves
outside the room where King stayed and just a few yards from the
concrete slab where he died. I found it very moving. After that,
visitors are ushered across the parking lot, through a short tunnel and
into an apartment building, where a couple flights up they’re standing
at the door of the bathroom where James Earl Ray took his shot. After
that experience, I was speechless for hours.

<span class="hide">                   </span>Presley was born in Tupelo,
Mississippi, but lived most of his life in Memphis. He was forty-two
when he died at his Memphis home, Graceland, on August 16, 1977. The
family’s apartment in Lauderdale Court, a formerly segregated public
housing project, is now owned and managed by Henry Turley’s company.

**172<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[now
home to FedEx and AutoZone](#Chapter7.html_ch7note36):** FedEx is
Memphis’s largest employer. Its chief executive officer, Fred Smith, is
a graduate of the Memphis University School, where the headmaster, Ellis
Haguewood, told me, “Without Fred Smith I don’t know where Memphis would
be.” AutoZone’s chief, Pitt Hyde, is also a graduate of Memphis
University School (as is Henry Turley).

<span id="Notes.html_page_256"></span>**173<span class="hide">     </span><span class="spacen3i"></span>[Wells
Fargo and Shelby-Memphis settled](#Chapter7.html_ch7note40):** Ted
Evanoff, “Leaders Say City Got Best Deal Possible from Bank,”
*Commercial Appeal*, June 3, 2012,
[www.commercialappeal.com/news/2012/jun/03/wells-fargo-goes-from-foe-to-partner/?print=1](http://www.commercialappeal.com/news/2012/jun/03/wells-fargo-goes-from-foe-to-partner/?print=1).
A Wells Fargo spokeswoman sent me these comments:

<span class="hide">                   </span>Wells Fargo strictly
prohibits discrimination based on race, age or other demographic
factors. We have practices and advocated for responsible home lending
and responsible loan servicing for a very long time.

<span class="hide">                   </span>According to the 2011 Home
Mortgage Disclosure Act report (the most recently available report),
Wells Fargo maintained its leadership in fair mortgage lending. We were
the No. 1 lender in the market overall, and in lending to all racial and
ethnic groups.

<span class="hide">                   </span>Wells Fargo was the No. 1
originator of home loans across the board in all key categories,
including loans to African-Americans, Asians, Hispanics, Native
Americans, low- and moderate-income borrowers, and residents of low- and
moderate-income neighborhoods.

<span class="hide">                   </span>Here are a few points
related to the agreement Wells Fargo has with Memphis and Shelby County.

<span class="hide">                   </span>Earlier this year (2012),
Wells Fargo and the City of Memphis and Shelby County all agreed that it
was in the best interest of all parties to end the litigation. The City
of Memphis and Shelby County agreed to drop their lawsuit against Wells
so that we could enter into a collaborative agreement for the benefit of
the community. In effect, allowing us to work together to help
communities in the area recover from the housing crisis. Memphis and
Shelby County are markets where our local bank officials are active
partners in the community.

<span class="hide">                   </span>Some of the details of the
agreement are:

<span class="hide">                </span><img src="images/bull-img.jpg" class="inline" /><span class="hide"><img src="images/bull-img.jpg" width="12" height="10" /></span><span class="hide">  </span><span class="spacebull"></span>Wells
Fargo will provide \$4.5 million for a home ownership
<span id="Notes.html_page_257"></span>program that will provide down
payment and renovation grants as well as home buyer education.

<span class="hide">                </span><img src="images/bull-img.jpg" class="inline" /><span class="hide"><img src="images/bull-img.jpg" width="12" height="10" /></span><span class="hide">  </span><span class="spacebull"></span>Wells
Fargo will contribute an additional \$3 million for local initiatives
related to improving economic vitality, preserving public safety, and
increasing financial literacy, with spending directed by the city and
county.

<span class="hide">                </span><img src="images/bull-img.jpg" class="inline" /><span class="hide"><img src="images/bull-img.jpg" width="12" height="10" /></span><span class="hide">  </span><span class="spacebull"></span>Wells
Fargo also communicated that we have made a \$425 million home purchase
lending goal for the city and county over the next five years.

<span class="hide">                 </span>From January of 2009 through
September of 2012, we have forgiven more than \$5.5 billion of mortgage
principal for our customers facing financial hardship. We have placed
more than 800,000 home owners into active trial and completed
modifications.

<span class="hide">                 </span>We are committed to putting
people in homes and helping them stay there.

<span id="Abouttheauthor.html"></span>

<span id="Abouttheauthor.html_page_276"></span><span id="Abouttheauthor.html_page_277"></span><img src="images/author.jpg" width="194" height="223" />

D<span class="small">AVE</span> C<span class="small">ROSS</span>

**B<span class="small">OB</span> I<span class="small">VRY</span>** is a
reporter for Bloomberg News whose awards include the 2009 George Polk
Award, a 2008 Gerald Loeb Award, a 2012 special citation from the
Goldsmith Investigative Reporting Award jury at Harvard’s Kennedy
School, the Society of Professional Journalists 2012 Public Service in
Online Journalism Award, the 2012 Investigative Reporters and Editors
Freedom of Information Prize, the 2010 Hillman Prize, three New York
Press Club Awards, two National Headliner Awards, and three awards from
the Society of American Business Editors and Writers. He’s written for
*Esquire*, *Maxim*, *Popular Science*, and *Washington Post Book World*;
and his short fiction has appeared in *Esquire* and *Ploughshares*.
Before joining Bloomberg News, Ivry worked for the *Record*, the *San
Francisco Examiner*, and the *San Francisco Bay Guardian*. In 2012,
[BusinessInsider.com](http://BusinessInsider.com) named
[@bobivry](http://www.Twitter.com/bobivry) one of “The 101 Finance
People You Have to Follow on Twitter.”

<span id="Aboutthepublisher.html"></span>

<span id="Aboutthepublisher.html_page_278"></span><img src="images/pub.jpg" width="62" height="53" />

PublicAffairs is a publishing house founded in 1997. It is a tribute to
the standards, values, and flair of three persons who have served as
mentors to countless reporters, writers, editors, and book people of all
kinds, including me.

I. F. S<span class="small">TONE</span>, proprietor of *I. F. Stone’s
Weekly*, combined a commitment to the First Amendment with
entrepreneurial zeal and reporting skill and became one of the great
independent journalists in American history. At the age of eighty, Izzy
published *The Trial of Socrates*, which was a national bestseller. He
wrote the book after he taught himself ancient Greek.

B<span class="small">ENJAMIN</span> C.
B<span class="small">RADLEE</span> was for nearly thirty years the
charismatic editorial leader of *The Washington Post*. It was Ben who
gave the *Post* the range and courage to pursue such historic issues as
Watergate. He supported his reporters with a tenacity that made them
fearless and it is no accident that so many became authors of
influential, best-selling books.

R<span class="small">OBERT</span> L.
B<span class="small">ERNSTEIN</span>, the chief executive of Random
House for more than a quarter century, guided one of the nation’s
premier publishing houses. Bob was personally responsible for many books
of political dissent and argument that challenged tyranny around the
globe. He is also the founder and longtime chair of Human Rights Watch,
one of the most respected human rights organizations in the world.

•      •      •

For fifty years, the banner of Public Affairs Press was carried by its
owner Morris B. Schnapper, who published Gandhi, Nasser, Toynbee,
Truman, and about 1,500 other authors. In 1983, Schnapper was described
by The *Washington Post* as “a redoubtable gadfly.” His legacy will
endure in the books to come.

<img src="images/sign.jpg" width="99" height="42" />

Peter Osnos, *Founder and Editor-at-Large*
